an extension in $S$, and no marker is a prefix of an interior
vertex of $S$.  Then
\[
     \operatorname{Int}(S)\cup R
\]
is also ambient meet-closed.
In fact the meet of any two \emph{distinct} nodes of this union
belongs to $\operatorname{Int}(S)$, by
\lean{DualTree.InteriorMarkerMeet.meet_distinct_interior_union_markers}.
The main theorem is
\lean{DualTree.InteriorMarkerMeet.meetClosed_interior_union_markers}.
The proof relies on the elementary but important fact that a
longest common prefix is unaffected by extending an input
beyond its first disagreement with the other input.
Next this abstract statement must be instantiated with the literal
$R$ of Lemma~27.  One must establish support extensions of all its
markers inside the original semi-complete starred tree, and check
the marker/base separation and antichain hypotheses from the
maximal-interior construction.
Even after that, meet-closure must be transported through the
actual projected leaf replacement to $S_w$.


\paragraph{Meet closure and direction uniqueness of semi-complete skew supports.} \todo
The complete-support meet-closure theorem previously used in the
canonical cone construction does not directly apply to the
semi-complete source support $S$ of a starred word, since its leaves
may have different intrinsic heights.
The module \lean{DualTree.SemiCompleteSkewMeet} removes that
restriction.  Semi-completeness forces the final skew branching
witness to have all $b$ immediate successors; every other non-leaf
occurs before that witness and has one successor in each direction.
Thus
\lean{DualTree.SemiCompleteSkewMeet.fullDirections_of_semiComplete_nonleaf_total}
establishes existence and uniqueness of all directions at any non-leaf,
while \lean{DualTree.SemiCompleteSkewMeet.meetClosed_starred}
shows that the original starred support $S$ is closed under
ambient longest-common-prefix meets.
The precise next step is to transport this meet geometry from
$S$ to the literal signature skeleton
$\operatorname{Int}(S')\cup\{t_0\}\cup R$
and then to its reconstructed image $S_w$.
These two transport implications, and the separate skew-order
conditions on $S_w$, remain to be checked.


\paragraph{Meet closure characterises unique successor directions in rooted supports.} \todo
The remaining semi-complete branching check for $S_w$ can also be
approached through ambient meets.  In a finite node set, two
distinct immediate successors of a vertex in the same direction
would have a common ambient meet strictly between the vertex
and at least one successor.  Thus meet closure rules out such a
pair, without a skewness assumption.
The reusable theorem
\lean{DualTree.MeetClosedDirectionConverse.atMostOneDirection_of_meetClosed}
formalizes this argument, and
\lean{DualTree.MeetClosedDirectionConverse.meetClosed_iff_atMostOneDirection_of_rooted}
shows that for rooted finite supports meet closure is equivalent
to having at most one immediate successor in each direction.
Applied to the already checked full-direction existence,
this would give exactly $b$ immediate successors.
The remaining *source-specific* condition is to prove that
the literal skeleton (equivalently its reconstructed $Q$ image
with preserved prefix directions) is ambient meet-closed.


\paragraph{Typed prefix-order embedding of the entire literal signature skeleton.} \todo
The preceding four-case prefix analysis is now packaged as an

The reconstructed map fixes the old base and sends each $s\in R$
to its designated projected $D_2$ point.
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QPrefixSkeleton.newNode_prefix_iff_original}
proves that the new labels preserve and reflect prefix ordering

\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QPrefixSkeleton.newNode_injective}

This identifies the rooted \emph{prefix-posets} of the literal
skeleton and its reconstructed image.  Preservation of the
additional ambient-length and auxiliary-order conditions in

embedding and remains a separate verification obligation.


\paragraph{Surjectivity of the typed literal-to-$Q$ prefix skeleton map.} \todo
The prefix-order embedding above was initially expressed on the

The module \lean{DualTree.Lemma27QPrefixSurjectivity} now verifies

\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QPrefixSurjectivity.originalNode_surjective_on_skeleton}

Conversely, the two

With the previously checked injectivity and prefix reflection,

\paragraph{All ambient successor directions are occupied in the reconstructed $Q$ tree.} \todo
The earlier direction-preservation result reduced full branching at
old interior nodes to the literal pre-replacement skeleton.  The new
module \lean{DualTree.Lemma27QFullDirections} proves the needed
existence.  For any original interior node $s\ne t_0$,
the verified full-before clause supplies a descendant of $S$
in each ambient direction.  Such a descendant is either an old
interior node, lies below the retained $t_0$, or is an exceptional
leaf whose first boundary marker lies in the same direction.
The last assertion is formalized by
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QFullDirections.exceptional_leaf_marker_in_direction}.
At $t_0$ the literal $R$ contains every immediate ambient child.
Thus \lean{DualTree.Lemma27QFullDirections.oldSkeleton_all_directions}
proves that all $b$ directions are occupied in the literal skeleton.
Combined with the exact direction comparison,
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QFullDirections.sourceQ_all_directions}
provides an actual $S_w$ descendant in each direction from every
vertex of $\operatorname{Int}(S)$.
This proves \emph{existence} in all $b$ directions. The remaining
semi-completeness obligation is the uniqueness of an immediate
$S_w$ successor within each such direction; the ambient
skew ordering and rank clauses must still be checked.



\paragraph{Occupied branching directions agree before and after $Q$ leaf replacement.} \todo
To verify the semi-complete successor condition it is not enough

\emph{ambient successor directions} represented at each old
interior node. The module
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QBranchOccupancy} defines the finite
literal pre-replacement skeleton
$\operatorname{Int}(S')\cup\{t_0\}\cup R$ and proves
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QBranchOccupancy.occupied_iff_under_Q}.
For every retained old base vertex $u$ and direction $i\in b$,
there is a vertex of $S_w$ inside the cone $u^\frown i$
if and only if the literal pre-replacement skeleton contains
such a vertex. The proof uses the exact bijection of literal
markers with $D_2$ coordinates and the verified direction
preservation for each replacement.
Thus no branch direction is introduced or lost by $Q$.
The remaining semi-completeness obligation is to show that
the literal pre-replacement skeleton actually has all $b$
successor directions at every original interior vertex,
and to verify the skew ordering clauses separately.



\paragraph{Preservation of the first branching direction below old interior nodes.} \todo
The prefix geometry proved above can be strengthened to retain the
*ambient direction* of the immediate branch from each old base
vertex.  The theorem
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QBranchDirections.childCone_prefix_iff_of_strict_prefix}
shows that prolonging a literal marker beyond itself does not
change which of the $b$ successor cones of a proper predecessor
contains that marker.  In the actual reconstructed $S_w$,
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QBranchDirections.oldBase_childCone_prefix_iff}
checks this equivalence for every node of
$\operatorname{Int}(S')\cup\{t_0\}$ and each literal $R$ marker.
This is a necessary step towards the semi-complete branching
axiom: the number of occupied directions can now be compared
before and after replacement.  It still remains to show that
all required directions are occupied, and to check the
ambient-length and auxiliary-order clauses of skewness.


\paragraph{Prefix geometry of replacing literal signature leaves.} \todo
The construction of $S_w$ replaces each terminal marker $s\in R$
with the projected node $P_{j(s)}(z_s)$, retaining the old base
$\operatorname{Int}(S')\cup\{t_0\}$.
The module \lean{DualTree.Lemma27QLeafReplacement} verifies that
this operation preserves the \emph{prefix relations} among these
two kinds of nodes.  The literal markers lie outside the inclusive
cut and are pairwise prefix-incomparable.
The theorem
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QLeafReplacement.oldBase_prefix_projection_iff}
shows that any old base vertex precedes a projected marker precisely
when it preceded its original marker.
The reverse prefix direction, from a projected marker to an old base
vertex, is impossible; and
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QLeafReplacement.projectedMarkers_prefix_iff}
checks the relation between pairs of projected markers.
These facts prepare a finite prefix-order isomorphism between the
old signature skeleton and its reconstructed image.
They do \emph{not} establish the ambient-length or auxiliary-order
conditions of the source's skew definition, nor the complete
successor-direction condition required for semi-completeness.


\paragraph{Equality of the reconstructed interior with the old interior.} \todo
The separate marker-coverage and terminal-leaf results finally
give both directions of the interior identity.  The module
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QInterior} shows that each old interior
node $s\ne t_0$ retains a strict descendant in $S_w$: first
use the corresponding immediate successor in $S'$, and if
that successor is an exceptional boundary, use its projected
$D_2$ descendant.  The distinguished cut $t_0$ similarly
keeps a strict descendant through an immediate child marker
when the branching number is positive.
Thus, together with the previously proved upper inclusion,
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QInterior.source_interior_iff_original}
establishes
\[
  \operatorname{Int}(S_w)=\operatorname{Int}(S)
\]
for the corrected common cone domain and $b>0$.
The proof does not rely on an assumed count of the interiors.
It uses the literal $R$ and the actual projective coordinate map,
so all descendants genuinely belong to $S_w$.
Still to verify: the full skew and semi-complete branching
properties of $S_w$, the source-compatible word $g_w$, and the
smooth colouring compatibility needed in Lemma~27.



\paragraph{Every literal signature marker survives in the $Q$ tree.} \todo
The marker sets relevant to the reconstruction are the *literal*
$R$ from Lemma~27, not just the candidate cones.  The module
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QMarkerCoverage} proves that both kinds of
markers used by the source are present:
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QMarkerCoverage.child_mem_literalR}
handles every ambient immediate successor of $t_0$, while
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QMarkerCoverage.boundaryMarker_mem_literalR}
handles each exceptional signature-leaf boundary at a maximal cut.
For each $s\in R$, its designated $D_2$ coordinate projects
a node extending $s$ by
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QMarkerCoverage.marker_prefix_projected_point}.
More importantly,
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QMarkerCoverage.literalMarker_has_Q_descendant}
places that projected descendant in the *actual reconstructed*
node set $S_w$, not just the complete support $T$.
These are the explicit marker-coverage facts required to show that
each retained original interior vertex has a strict descendant in
$S_w$.  That interior-persistence argument and subsequent
semi-completeness verification remain separate obligations.



\paragraph{New projected $Q$ markers are leaves.} \todo
Every marked point $P_i(s_i)$ lies strictly after the auxiliary
cut and belongs to a frontier cone disjoint from all other
marked cones.  The previously verified old signature base lies
at or before the cut.  The module \lean{DualTree.Lemma27QTerminal}
combines these facts to show that no node of
$S_w$ is a proper extension of any marked point $P_i(s_i)$.
The theorem
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QTerminal.markedProjection_not_interior}
therefore certifies that all new marked points are actual leaves
of the reconstructed finite node set.  Consequently
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QTerminal.sourceSignatureNodes_interior_subset_original}
establishes
\[
  \operatorname{Int}(S_w)\subseteq\operatorname{Int}(S).
\]
The reverse inclusion is not inferred from cardinality alone:
it requires proving that every original interior node keeps
a strict descendant, using the complete set of literal
signature markers and their corresponding projected cones.
Thus equality of interiors and semi-completeness of $S_w$
are still open proof obligations.



\paragraph{Exact literal $R$ count of the $Q$ skeleton.} \todo
The coordinate partition has already been defined by an injection
from the literal signature markers $R$ into the frontier coordinates.
The module \lean{DualTree.Lemma27QMarkerCount} uses the two
finite equivalences
$\operatorname{Fin}(|R|)\cong R\cong D_2$
(the first from the duplicate-free literal marker list, the
second from the constructed bullet-coordinate indexing).
This gives the genuine equality $|D_2|=|R|$ in
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QMarkerCount.bullet_card_eq_literalR_length}.
Combined with the exact no-collision result for $S_w$, the theorem
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QMarkerCount.sourceSignatureNodes_card_eq_l_add_literalR}
proves
\[
   |S_w|=l+|R|.
\]
This checks the cardinality of the reconstructed *node set*;
the intrinsic interior, semi-completeness of $S_w$, and the
starred word $g_w$ still require separate validation.



\paragraph{Exact size and support membership of the reconstructed $Q$ tree.} \todo
The previous proof of the old signature interior now combines with

early-base hypothesis.
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QTreeCount.oldBase_mem_iff_original_interior}
establishes equality of the *sets*
$\operatorname{Int}(S')\cup\{t_0\}$ and $\operatorname{Int}(S)$.
Consequently the old base has precisely $l$ nodes, as certified by
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QTreeCount.oldBase_card}.
Theorem
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QTreeCount.sourceSignatureNodes_subset_support}
proves that every vertex of the concrete $Q$ tree component lies in
the original complete support $T$, and
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27QTreeCount.sourceSignatureNodes_card}
gives the exact cardinality
\[
 |S_w|=l+|D_2|.
\]
The proof uses the fact that the old base is at or before the cut
and the new marked nodes lie strictly after it and in pairwise
disjoint frontier cones.  These are source-specific, unconditional
consequences of the existing complete-skew and maximal-cut
hypotheses.  They do not yet prove the semi-complete property of

\paragraph{Unique global cone-coordinate lookup for rootwise coding.} \todo
The repaired clauses defining $Q$ and the final refinement $f'$
refer to a pair $(i,z)$ with a source variable root

The module \lean{DualTree.Lemma27ConeIndex} introduces the

\lean{DualTree.Lemma27ConeIndex.projectSlot_injective}

The partial inverse
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27ConeIndex.locateSlot}
returns the unique pair when a support node lies in the

The theorems
\lean{DualTree.Lemma27ConeIndex.locateSlot_spec},

\lean{DualTree.Lemma27ConeIndex.locateSlot_none_iff}
verify these claims and the exact fallback criterion.
This is the address-selection interface needed to define
$g_w$ by a uniform substitution on the roots of the original
word.  It still does not assemble that substitution, prove
its admissibility, or establish the smoothness of $c\circ Q$.




\paragraph{Recovering local addresses of support roots inside a cone.} \todo
The previous canonical cone construction verifies the forward

The module \lean{DualTree.InverseConeTails} defines this address
by removing $I_T^{-1}(t_i)$ from the beginning of $I_T^{-1}(t)$.
Prefix reflection and the rank equation show that this is exact

The central theorem

\lean{DualTree.InverseConeTails.projectCommon_injective}

This is the necessary interface for matching old variable roots to

construction of the global variable-fibre substitution.


\paragraph{Cone-local word changes and smooth colouring compatibility.} \todo
The module \lean{DualTree.ConeLocalPatchSmoothness} explicitly modifies
a mixed-product word only within the inclusive cone of its marked
$D_2$ point, leaving all plain-coordinate words and all letters
outside marked cones fixed.
\lean{DualTree.MixedProduct.patchedElement_smooth}
proves that any two such elements with the same background words and
marked points are related by the $\Gamma_2=D_2$ instance of
Definition~21's smoothness comparison.  Consequently
\lean{DualTree.MixedProduct.smoothColor_invariant_under_patches}
shows that a colouring genuinely constant on these smoothness
classes must ignore arbitrary changes inside these cones.

The concrete kernel-checked theorem
\lean{DualTree.BulletSmoothnessAudit.markedLetterColor_not_smooth}
exhibits two smoothly related mixed-product elements with
*different* letters at their common marked point.  Hence the natural
colouring which simply remembers the marked letter is not automatically
smooth.  This is a counterexample to an \emph{unqualified smoothness
claim for arbitrary induced colourings}, not a counterexample to
Lemma~27.  In particular, a proposed total extension of the colouring
$c\circ Q$ must either demonstrate classwise compatibility on its
starred domain or alter the coding so this obstruction is avoided.
The requirement is not discharged merely by choosing the $D_2$
coordinates or constructing a well-typed mixed element.

These lemmas are groundwork for the semi-completeness and

\paragraph{Directional construction of the canonical support walk.} \todo
\lean{DualTree.CanonicalSupportWalk.next} selects the immediate
support successor in a given ambient direction whenever such a
successor exists uniquely; the membership, immediate-successor,
and direction assertions are verified by
\lean{DualTree.CanonicalSupportWalk.next_unique} and
\lean{DualTree.CanonicalSupportWalk.next_mem}.
Iterating this transition defines a concrete total map from finite
$b$-ary addresses to ambient nodes.  The associativity of traversal,
root containment, and initial-segment preservation are proved by
\lean{DualTree.CanonicalSupportWalk.walk_append},
\lean{DualTree.CanonicalSupportWalk.walk_root_prefix}, and
\lean{DualTree.CanonicalSupportWalk.walk_prefix}.
The first source direction determines the ambient cone whenever
its successor exists uniquely, by
\lean{DualTree.CanonicalSupportWalk.walk_first_direction}.
The image belongs to the support for any source path for which all
intermediate directional successors are present, by
\lean{DualTree.CanonicalSupportWalk.walk_mem_of_admissible}.
The resulting cone projections are prefix-preserving by
\lean{DualTree.CanonicalSupportWalk.projection_walk_prefix}.

This makes the address recursion explicit without assuming an
abstract prefix-preserving $I_T$.  The canonical root is now selected
from any complete skew support, as recorded next.  To identify the
walk with the precise source map $I_T$ it remains to prove that
every address in $b^{<k}$ has a valid support path, establish
the intrinsic-height identity, and prove the inverse/coordinate
identities on its image.
The totalized fall-back at a missing branch is not asserted to
be injective and cannot substitute for these steps.


\paragraph{Exact intrinsic rank at an immediate support successor.} \todo
The module \lean{DualTree.ImmediateSupportHeight} proves that the
proper support predecessors of an immediate successor $t$ of $s$
are exactly the proper predecessors of $s$ together with $s$.
The finite cardinality argument gives
$h_T(t)=h_T(s)+1$ (without any skewness assumption), by
\lean{DualTree.ImmediateSupportHeight.heightAt_immediate}.
The support-root equation $h_T(r)=0$ is also certified by
\lean{DualTree.ImmediateSupportHeight.heightAt_root_zero}.
Consequently
\lean{DualTree.ImmediateSupportHeight.heightAt_next}
supplies the rank increment for each admissible step in the
constructive address walk.  The induction over bounded addresses
is supplied by the module
\lean{DualTree.CompleteSupportAddresses}, as detailed below.
The inverse and unique-address properties remain open.

\paragraph{All bounded addresses in a complete skew support.} \todo
The theorem \lean{DualTree.CompleteSupportAddresses.admissible_and_height}
proves by induction on a source address $u$ that, whenever
$h_T(s)+|u|<k$, the support walk beginning at $s$ is admissible
and its endpoint has intrinsic height $h_T(s)+|u|$.
Its crucial induction steps are uniqueness of every branch below
the final intrinsic level, and the exact rank increase at each
immediate successor.  Specializing to the chosen common support root
gives \lean{DualTree.CompleteSupportAddresses.canonical_bounded_address}:
every $u\in b^{<k}$ has an admissible trace, belongs to the image of
the concrete support walk, and satisfies
$h_T(I_T(u))=|u|$.
The bundled map \lean{DualTree.CompleteSupportAddresses.canonicalEmbedding}
is now defined on all of $b^{<k}$ with values in $T$; its height
identity and prefix preservation are separately audited by
\lean{DualTree.CompleteSupportAddresses.canonicalEmbedding_height}
and \lean{DualTree.CompleteSupportAddresses.canonicalEmbedding_prefix}.
Injectivity of the map is now reduced to the pathwise
uniqueness theorem, as recorded next.  Surjectivity,
existence of an address for every support vertex, and the resulting
exact projection identities remain open.



\paragraph{Injectivity of the canonical support walk.} \todo
For two admissible source addresses with equal images, the first
directions (if any) must coincide: the images lie in their respective
disjoint ambient successor cones.  Removing that common direction
reduces to shorter admissible addresses.  This is formalized as
\lean{DualTree.CanonicalSupportInjective.walk_injective_of_admissible};
the empty/nonempty cases use the strictness of the first successor.
Consequently the concrete map from $b^{<k}$ into $T$ is injective,
as certified by
\lean{DualTree.CanonicalSupportInjective.canonicalEmbedding_injective}
using the previously validated full-admissibility theorem.
The remaining geometric step is surjectivity onto $T$,
or equivalently an inverse address for each support node,
followed by the source-specific cone-map identities.


\paragraph{Reaching every support vertex by a source address.} \todo
The finite ascent theorem
\lean{DualTree.SupportReachability.walk_address_of_prefix}
builds an admissible address for any pair of support nodes $s\sqsubseteq t$.
At a strict step it selects the first immediate successor $u$ below
$t$, identifies its direction, and recurses from $u$ to $t$.
The distance $|t|-|s|$ strictly decreases, so the construction terminates.
Uniqueness of the chosen direction follows from completeness and
the skew branching clauses.  The lemma
\lean{DualTree.SupportReachability.walk_height_of_admissible}
proves that the length of an admissible address equals the increase
of intrinsic rank.  Therefore
\lean{DualTree.SupportReachability.support_has_address} gives every
$t\in T$ an admissible source address of length $h_T(t)$
whose image under the root-anchored walk is $t$.
This is a surjectivity statement for the unrestricted admissible walk.
The rank bound on every support vertex and the inverse construction
are now addressed by \lean{DualTree.CompleteSupportSurjective}
as detailed in the next paragraph.


\paragraph{Surjectivity and the inverse canonical address map.} \todo
The finite-support lemma
\lean{DualTree.CompleteSupportSurjective.exists_terminal_above}
extends every support vertex to a terminal support vertex, using
strict growth of the intrinsic rank as a terminating induction
measure.  The complete-support leaf condition then gives
\lean{DualTree.CompleteSupportSurjective.heightAt_lt_k}:
$h_T(t)<k$ for every $t\in T$.
Combined with the admissible address construction, this proves
that the concrete map from $b^{<k}$ onto $T$ is surjective,
and hence bijective, by
\lean{DualTree.CompleteSupportSurjective.canonicalEmbedding_surjective}
and \lean{DualTree.CompleteSupportSurjective.canonicalEmbedding_bijective}.
The inverse choice
\lean{DualTree.CompleteSupportSurjective.inverseAddress}
comes with both inverse identities, as well as
\lean{DualTree.CompleteSupportSurjective.inverse_length},
which establishes $|I_T^{-1}(t)|=h_T(t)$.
These are the remaining underlying domain/coordinate facts for
the canonical cones of Lemma~27.
Still to verify: the source's exact auxiliary-order compatibility,
the final global coding $Q$, the smooth-coloring argument and
the complete-skew geometry of the selected refinement.


\paragraph{Prefix order isomorphism of the canonical map.} \todo
The bijection $I_T:b^{<k}\to T$ preserves initial segments,
but a separate reflection proof is needed to assert that it is
an isomorphism of rooted trees.  The theorem
\lean{DualTree.CanonicalSupportPrefixIso.canonicalEmbedding_prefix_reflect}
takes a support prefix $I_T(u)\sqsubseteq I_T(v)$, chooses the
prefix of $v$ at depth $|u|$, and compares the two support images
using uniqueness of a support predecessor at a prescribed intrinsic
height.  Injectivity then identifies that prefix with $u$.
Thus \lean{DualTree.CanonicalSupportPrefixIso.canonicalEmbedding_prefix_iff}
proves equivalence of both initial-segment relations, and
\lean{DualTree.CanonicalSupportPrefixIso.inverseAddress_prefix}
checks initial-segment preservation by the canonical inverse.
This concerns the \emph{prefix tree} structure only; the separate
printed auxiliary-order counterexample must not be silently
discarded or reinterpreted as a verified order-preservation theorem.


\paragraph{Concrete cone maps on the intrinsic safe domain.} \todo
With the canonical prefix-tree isomorphism now explicit,
\lean{DualTree.CanonicalConeCoordinates.SourceTail} defines the
tail domain at a support frontier $t$ by
$|I_T^{-1}(t)|+|z|<k$.  This yields the actual typed projection
$P_t(z)=I_T(I_T^{-1}(t)^\frown z)$ in
